# FC05 — Weibel, *An Introduction to Homological Algebra*

Frozen source: ``Weibel - 1994 - An Introduction to Homological Algebra.md``

Scope: complete book, Chapters 1–10 and Appendix A; exercises excluded item-by-item. Stable source-unit IDs are in source order and are the identifiers retained by later foundational-corpus sweeps.

Coverage: 1059 source units. Chapter counts: C01=68, C02=82, C03=66, C04=86, C05=80, C06=154, C07=80, C08=106, C09=164, C10=121, CA=52.

| `FC05-C01-U001` | **Definition/convention — Exactness for module sequences**                                                  | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L255–261   | Fix an associative ring R; mod-R denotes right R-modules. A sequence A→B→C is exact at B when ker(g)=im(f).                                                                      | —                                                 |
| `FC05-C01-U002` | **Definition 1.1.1 — Chain complex; cycles, boundaries, homology**                                          | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L263–269   | A chain complex has d_n:C_n→C_{n-1} with d²=0; Z_n=ker d_n, B_n=im d_{n+1}, H_n=Z_n/B_n.                                                                                         | `FC05-C01-U001`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U003` | **Construction/category — Category Ch(mod-R) and chain maps**                                               | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L273–277   | Chain maps are degreewise maps commuting with differentials; composition is degreewise, giving Ch(mod-R).                                                                        | `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U004` | **Definition 1.1.2 — Quasi-isomorphism**                                                                    | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L285       | A chain map is a quasi-isomorphism when it induces isomorphisms on every homology group.                                                                                         | `FC05-C01-U002`, `FC05-C01-U003`                  |
| `FC05-C01-U005` | **Definition/convention — Cochain complexes and cohomology**                                                | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L293       | Reindex C^n=C_{-n}; define cocycles, coboundaries, H^n, cochain maps, and quasi-isomorphisms dually.                                                                             | `FC05-C01-U002`, `FC05-C01-U004`                  |
| `FC05-C01-U006` | **Definition/convention — Bounded complexes and standard subcategories**                                    | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L295–297   | Defines bounded, bounded above/below, amplitude [a,b], and Ch_b, Ch_-, Ch_+, Ch_{≥0}; gives cochain analogues.                                                                   | `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U007` | **Application 1.1.3 — Simplicial homology**                                                                 | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L301       | For a geometric simplicial complex, the alternating face map gives a simplicial chain complex; its homology is simplicial homology.                                              | `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U008` | **Application 1.1.4 — Singular homology**                                                                   | §1.1; pp. 1–4; L305–311   | The free module on singular k-simplices with alternating face differential gives S_*(X); H_n(X;R)=H_n(S_*(X)), with simplicial-to-singular quasi-isomorphism for triangulated X. | `FC05-C01-U002`, `FC05-C01-U004`                  |
| `FC05-C01-U009` | **Definitions — Ab-categories and additive functors**                                                       | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L317–325   | An Ab-category has abelian-group Hom sets and bilinear composition; an additive functor preserves addition on Hom groups.                                                        | —                                                 |
| `FC05-C01-U010` | **Definition/construction — Additive category; zero object, products and coproducts**                       | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L327–333   | An additive category is an Ab-category with finite products and coproducts; in Ch these are degreewise, hence Ch is additive.                                                    | `FC05-C01-U009`, `FC05-C01-U003`                  |
| `FC05-C01-U011` | **Definitions/constructions — Subcomplexes, quotient complexes, degreewise kernels/cokernels**              | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L337–343   | A subcomplex is degreewise stable under d; quotient differentials descend, and kernels/cokernels of chain maps are formed degreewise.                                            | `FC05-C01-U002`, `FC05-C01-U003`                  |
| `FC05-C01-U012` | **Definition 1.2.1 — Categorical kernel, cokernel, monic and epic**                                         | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L345       | Defines kernels and cokernels by universal properties in an additive category; monic/epi mean zero kernel/cokernel.                                                              | `FC05-C01-U010`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U013` | **Definition 1.2.2 — Abelian category and image factorization**                                             | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L354–360   | An abelian category is additive, has kernels/cokernels, and every mono/epi is a kernel/cokernel; im(f)=ker(coker f), yielding epi–mono factorization.                            | `FC05-C01-U012`                                   |
| `FC05-C01-U014` | **Definitions/conventions — Exact sequences and complexes in an abelian category**                          | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L372–376   | Exactness is defined by im=ker; defines abelian subcategory and extends chain complexes, maps, and homology to any abelian category A.                                           | `FC05-C01-U013`, `FC05-C01-U002`                  |

| `FC05-C01-U016` | **Example 1.2.4 — Double complexes**                                                                        | §1.2; pp. 5–9; L386–400   | A bicomplex C_{p,q} has horizontal/vertical differentials squaring to zero and anticommuting; rows/columns are complexes; bounded and first-quadrant variants are named.         | `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |

| `FC05-C03-U062` | **Theorem 3.6.3 — Künneth formula for tensor products of complexes**                        | §3.6; pp. 87–90; L2687–2695 | For P,Q with P_n and d(P_n) flat, gives the direct-sum H_p(P)⊗H_q(Q) term, Tor_1 correction, and noncanonical splitting over Z with P free.                                                 | `FC05-C02-U074`, `FC05-C03-U060`                                                    |

| `FC05-C04-U001` | **Definitions 4.1.1 — Projective, injective, and flat dimensions**                                       | §4.1; pp. 91–94; L2761–2781                                                   | For a right R-module A, pd(A), id(A), and fd(A) are the minimum lengths of projective, injective, and flat resolutions respectively, or infinity if no finite such resolution exists.                                                                  | `FC05-C02-U015`, `FC05-C02-U027`, `FC05-C03-U018`                                                    |

| `FC05-C04-U068` | **Construction — Koszul complex**                                                                        | §4.5; pp. 111–114; L3350–3360                                                 | For central x∈R, K(x) is 0→R→^x R→0 in degrees 1,0 with generator e_x; for a finite central sequence x=(x_1,…,x_n), K(x) is the total tensor product K(x_1)⊗…⊗K(x_n).                                                                                  | `FC05-C02-U074`                                                                                      |

| `FC05-C04-U073` | **Corollary 4.5.4 — Acyclicity of Koszul homology on a regular sequence**                                | §4.5; pp. 111–114; L3422–3430                                                 | If x is a regular sequence on A, H_q(x,A)=0 for q≠0 and H_0(x,A)=A/xA.                                                                                                                                                                                 | `FC05-C04-U050`, `FC05-C04-U072`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C04-U074` | **Corollary 4.5.5 — Koszul resolution**                                                                  | §4.5; pp. 111–114; L3432–3442                                                 | If x is an R-regular sequence and I=(x_1,…,x_n), K(x) is a finite free resolution of R/I; hence Tor and Ext against R/I are computed by Koszul homology/cohomology.                                                                                    | `FC05-C04-U073`, `FC05-C04-U070`, `FC05-C02-U056`, `FC05-C02-U048`                                   |

| `FC05-C04-U076` | **Definition 4.6.1 — Local cohomology with respect to a finitely generated ideal**                       | §4.6; pp. 115–119; L3470–3485                                                 | For finitely generated I in commutative R, H_I^0(A)={a:I^i a=0 for some i}=colim Hom(R/I^i,A); define H_I^q=R^qH_I^0, equivalently colim Ext_R^q(R/I^i,A).                                                                                             | `FC05-C02-U047`, `FC05-C02-U069`, `FC05-C02-U048`                                                    |
| `FC05-C04-U077` | **Generalization 4.6.2 — Cohomology with supports**                                                      | §4.6; pp. 115–119; L3504–3522                                                 | For closed Z⊂X, H_Z^0(X,F) is global sections supported on Z and H_Z^n its right derived functors; on Spec(R), this extends H_I^n, with the stated punctured-spectrum long exact sequence and cohomology comparison.                                   | `FC05-C01-U056`, `FC05-C02-U051`, `FC05-C04-U076`                                                    |
| `FC05-C04-U078` | **Theorem 4.6.3 — Grade detected by local cohomology**                                                   | §4.6; pp. 115–119; L3528–3537                                                 | For commutative noetherian local (R,m) and finitely generated A, G(A) is the least n with H_m^n(A)≠0.                                                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C04-U055`, `FC05-C04-U076`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C04-U081` | **Definition 4.6.5 — Koszul-limit local cohomology**                                                     | §4.6; pp. 115–119; L3572                                                      | Defines H_x^q(A)=colim_i H^q(x^i,A).                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `FC05-C04-U080`, `FC05-C02-U060`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C04-U085` | **Corollary 4.6.9 — Vanishing range for local cohomology**                                               | §4.6; pp. 115–119; L3620–3626                                                 | For noetherian local R, H_m^q(A) can be nonzero only for G(A)≤q≤dim(R); if R is Cohen–Macaulay, H_m^q(R)≠0 exactly for q=dim(R).                                                                                                                       | `FC05-C04-U078`, `FC05-C04-U084`, `FC05-C04-U050`                                                    |

## Chapter 5. Spectral Sequences

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                           | Location                                                             | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Earlier FC05 dependencies                                                           |
| --------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `FC05-C05-U001` | **Construction — First pages of the double-complex spectral sequence**                       | §5.1 Introduction; pp. 120–121; L3646–3652                           | For a first-quadrant double complex E, set E¹_{pq}=H_q^v(E_{p,*}) and E²_{pq}=H_p^h(E¹_{*,q}); these are the first successive approximations to H_{p+q}(Tot E).                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C01-U016`, `FC05-C01-U018`, `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |
| `FC05-C05-U002` | **Definition 5.2.1 — Homology spectral sequence**                                            | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3685–3697                            | In an abelian category A, a homology spectral sequence from E^a is a family E^r_{pq} with d^r:E^r_{pq}→E^r_{p-r,q+r-1}, (d^r)^2=0, and specified isomorphisms E^{r+1}*{pq}≅H(E^r,d^r)*{pq}; morphisms commute with d and induce the next-page maps.                                                                       | `FC05-C01-U013`, `FC05-C01-U014`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U003` | **Example 5.2.2 — First-quadrant spectral sequence**                                         | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3699                                 | A homology spectral sequence is first quadrant when E^r_{pq}=0 unless p,q≥0. Then each fixed spot stabilizes for large r; its stable value is denoted E^∞_{pq}.                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C05-U002`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U004` | **Dual Definition 5.2.3 — Cohomology spectral sequence**                                     | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3701–3709                            | A cohomology spectral sequence has d_r^{pq}:E_r^{pq}→E_r^{p+r,q-r+1}, hence raises total degree by one, and E_{r+1} is the homology of E_r; equivalently E_r^{pq}=E^r_{-p,-q} for a homology spectral sequence.                                                                                                           | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C01-U005`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U005` | **Mapping Lemma 5.2.4**                                                                      | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3711                                 | A morphism of spectral sequences that is an isomorphism in every bidegree on one page r is an isomorphism on every later page s≥r.                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C05-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U006` | **Definition 5.2.5 — Bounded spectral sequence and convergence**                             | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3713–3731                            | Degree-bounded means finitely many nonzero E^a_{pq} in each total degree, hence every spot stabilizes. Such a spectral sequence converges to H_* when H_n has a finite filtration with E^∞*{pq}≅F_pH*{p+q}/F_{p-1}H_{p+q}; dually for cohomology.                                                                         | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C05-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U007` | **Example 5.2.6 — First-quadrant filtrations and edge maps**                                 | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3733–3745                            | For a first-quadrant spectral sequence converging to H_*, H_n has filtration 0=F_{-1}⊂…⊂F_n=H_n; the y-axis gives F_0 and the x-axis gives H_n/F_{n-1}, producing the standard edge homomorphisms. The cohomological version is dual.                                                                                     | `FC05-C05-U003`, `FC05-C05-U006`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U008` | **Definition 5.2.7 — Collapse of a spectral sequence**                                       | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3747                                 | A spectral sequence collapses at E^r when E^r has exactly one nonzero row or column; if it converges, the unique E^r_{pq} of total degree n identifies with H_n.                                                                                                                                                          | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C05-U006`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U009` | **Construction 5.2.8 — E^∞ via nested cycles and boundaries**                                | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3763–3777                            | For each bidegree there are nested subobjects B^r⊂B^{r+1}⊂Z^{r+1}⊂Z^r of E^a with E^r≅Z^r/B^r. Define B^∞=∪_rB^r, Z^∞=∩_rZ^r and E^∞=Z^∞/B^∞ when these exist; in bounded sequences they equal the stable page.                                                                                                           | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C03-U045`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U010` | **Definition 5.2.9 — Bounded-below spectral sequence**                                       | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3781–3783                            | A homology spectral sequence is bounded below when, in every total degree n, E^a_{pq}=0 for all sufficiently small p; dually, a cohomology spectral sequence is bounded below when the terms of degree n vanish for sufficiently large p.                                                                                 | `FC05-C05-U002`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U011` | **Definition 5.2.10 — Regular spectral sequence**                                            | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3785                                 | A spectral sequence is regular when for each (p,q) the outgoing differentials are zero for all sufficiently large r; equivalently Z^∞*{pq}=Z^r*{pq} eventually.                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C05-U002`, `FC05-C05-U009`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U012` | **Definition 5.2.11 — Weak convergence, abutment, and convergence**                          | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3787–3799                            | Weak convergence to H_* means a filtration F_pH_n with E^∞*{pq}≅F_pH*{p+q}/F_{p-1}H_{p+q}. It approaches H_* if the filtration is exhaustive and Hausdorff, and converges if additionally the spectral sequence is regular and H_n≅lim H_n/F_pH_n; bounded-below approach already implies convergence.                    | `FC05-C05-U009`, `FC05-C05-U011`, `FC05-C02-U063`                                   |
| `FC05-C05-U013` | **Comparison Theorem 5.2.12**                                                                | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3801                                 | For convergent spectral sequences E,E' with compatible h:H_*→H'_* and f:E→E', if f is an isomorphism on one page (hence on E^∞), then h is an isomorphism.                                                                                                                                                                | `FC05-C05-U005`, `FC05-C05-U012`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U014` | **Construction 5.2.13 — Multiplicative spectral sequence**                                   | §5.2 Terminology; pp. 122–126; L3813–3825                            | A bigraded product on E^a satisfying the graded Leibniz rule for d^a descends to every later page, yielding a multiplicative spectral sequence.                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C05-U002`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U015` | **Definition 5.3.1 — Serre fibration**                                                       | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3831–3843      | A based sequence F→E→B is a Serre fibration when F is the fiber over the basepoint and π:E→B has the homotopy-lifting property for every finite polyhedron P and homotopy P×I→B; the section assumes B simply connected.                                                                                                  | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C05-U016` | **Theorem 5.3.2 — Leray–Serre spectral sequence**                                            | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3845–3873      | For a Serre fibration F→E→B with B simply connected, there is a first-quadrant homology spectral sequence E²_{pq}=H_p(B;H_q(F))⇒H_{p+q}(E). Its edge maps are induced by F→E and E→B (under the stated connectivity hypothesis on F for the latter).                                                                      | `FC05-C05-U015`, `FC05-C05-U003`, `FC05-C05-U006`, `FC05-C01-U008`                  |
| `FC05-C05-U017` | **Application 5.3.3 — Low-degree exact sequence of a fibration**                             | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3875–3889      | The lower-left corner of the Leray–Serre sequence gives an exact homology sequence through H_2(F), H_2(E), H_2(B), H_1(F), H_1(E), with d²:H_2(B)→H_1(F); the preceding term records the first obstruction to a homology LES for a fibration.                                                                             | `FC05-C05-U016`, `FC05-C05-U007`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U018` | **Application 5.3.4 — Loop-space fibration**                                                 | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3891–3897      | For ΩB→PB→B with PB contractible and π_1(B)=0, the Leray–Serre sequence yields H_1(ΩB)≅H_2(B) and the displayed exact sequence H_4(B)→H_2(B)⊗H_2(B)→H_2(ΩB)→H_3(B)→0.                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C05-U015`, `FC05-C05-U016`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U019` | **Application 5.3.5 — Wang sequence**                                                        | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3905–3911      | For a fibration F→E→S^n with n≠0,1, only the p=0,n columns survive on E², giving the Wang long exact sequence …→H_q(F)→H_q(E)→H_{q-n}(F)→^{d^n}H_{q-1}(F)→…; in particular H_q(F)≅H_q(E) for 0≤q≤n−2.                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C05-U016`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U020` | **Example 5.3.6 — Homology of SO(3) from the fibration over S²**                             | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3927–3939      | Applying the Wang sequence to S¹→SO(3)→S² and using π_1SO(3)=Z/2 gives H_3(SO(3))≅Z, H_1(SO(3))≅Z/2, and H_2(SO(3))=0 (the extraction’s apparent Z is an OCR error contradicted by exactness).                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C05-U019`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U021` | **Application 5.3.7 — Gysin sequence**                                                       | §5.3 The Leray-Serre Spectral Sequence; pp. 127–130; L3941–3947      | For a sphere fibration S^n→E→B with B simply connected and n≠0, the two-row Leray–Serre sequence yields the Gysin long exact sequence …→H_{p-n}(B)→H_p(E)→H_p(B)→^{d^{n+1}}H_{p-n-1}(B)→…; hence H_p(E)≅H_p(B) for p<n.                                                                                                   | `FC05-C05-U016`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U022` | **Definition — Filtration of a chain complex**                                               | §5.4 Spectral Sequence of a Filtration; pp. 131–134; L3959           | A filtration F on C is an increasing family of chain subcomplexes …⊂F_{p-1}C⊂F_pC⊂… .                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C01-U002`, `FC05-C01-U011`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U023` | **Definition — Exhaustive filtration**                                                       | §5.4 Spectral Sequence of a Filtration; pp. 131–134; L3961           | A filtration F on C is exhaustive when C=∪_pF_pC; replacing C by ∪F_pC does not change the associated spectral sequence.                                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C05-U022`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U024` | **Construction Theorem 5.4.1 — Spectral sequence of a filtered complex**                     | §5.4 Spectral Sequence of a Filtration; pp. 131–134; L3963–3965      | A filtration F on a chain complex C canonically yields a spectral sequence with E^0_{pq}=F_pC_{p+q}/F_{p-1}C_{p+q} and E^1_{pq}=H_{p+q}(E^0_{p,*}).                                                                                                                                                                       | `FC05-C05-U022`, `FC05-C05-U002`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U025` | **Definition 5.4.2 — Bounded filtration**                                                    | §5.4 Spectral Sequence of a Filtration; pp. 131–134; L3967           | F is bounded when for each degree n there are s<t with F_sC_n=0 and F_tC_n=C_n; its associated spectral sequence is bounded.                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C05-U022`, `FC05-C05-U006`                                                    |

| `FC05-C05-U074` | **Definition — Bounded-below exact couple**                                                  | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4749                               | An exact couple is bounded below when for each total degree n there is f(n) with D_{pq}=0 whenever p<f(n); then the associated spectral sequence is bounded below.                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C05-U073`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U075` | **Classical Convergence Theorem 5.9.7**                                                      | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4751–4757                          | A bounded-below exact couple has a bounded-below spectral sequence converging to H_*=colim D. If in each total degree some D_{p,n-p}→H_n is an isomorphism, the spectral sequence is bounded and convergent.                                                                                                              | `FC05-C05-U074`, `FC05-C05-U073`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U076` | **Application 5.9.8 — Exact couple from Tor of an exact complex**                            | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4761–4779                          | For an exact complex C of left R-modules with cycles Z_p and a right module M, the long exact Tor sequences of 0→Z_p→C_p→Z_{p-1}→0 form an exact couple with E^0_{pq}=Tor_q(M,C_p) and E^1_{pq}=H_p(Tor_q(M,C_*)). If C is bounded below, the associated hyperhomology sequence converges to zero.                        | `FC05-C05-U068`, `FC05-C02-U056`, `FC05-C01-U022`, `FC05-C05-U058`                  |
| `FC05-C05-U077` | **Construction 5.9.9 — Bockstein spectral sequence**                                         | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4781–4804                          | Fix a prime ℓ. Any long exact sequence …→E_{n+1}→H_n→^ℓH_n→E_n→… forms an exact couple whose associated spectral sequence starts at E^0=E; standard examples come from mod-ℓ homology, homotopy, group homology, or C/ℓC.                                                                                                 | `FC05-C05-U068`, `FC05-C05-U070`                                                    |
| `FC05-C05-U078` | **Proposition 5.9.10 — Bockstein pages and E^∞**                                             | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4806–4816                          | For the Bockstein exact couple, each E_n^r fits into the stated exact sequence between a quotient of H_n and the ℓ^r-divisible ℓ-torsion in H_{n-1}; at E^∞ this becomes 0→H_n/(ℓH_n+T_n)→E_n^∞→Q_{n-1}→0, with T_n the ℓ-primary torsion and Q_n the infinitely ℓ-divisible part.                                        | `FC05-C05-U077`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U079` | **Corollary 5.9.11 — Bockstein convergence for finitely generated H**                        | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4832–4836                          | If every H_n is finitely generated and d_n=dim_Q(H_n⊗Q), then the Bockstein sequence stabilizes and E_n^∞≅(Z/ℓ)^{d_n}.                                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC05-C05-U078`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C05-U080` | **Corollary 5.9.12 — Reading ℓ-primary summands from Bockstein survival**                    | §5.9 Exact Couples; pp. 153–159; L4838–4842                          | Classes surviving to E^r but not E^{r+1} detect exponent-ℓ classes in H_{n-1} divisible by ℓ^r but not ℓ^{r+1}; if j(y) dies precisely on page r, then y generates a direct summand Z/ℓ^r of H_n.                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C05-U078`, `FC05-C05-U079`                                                    |

## Chapter 6. Group Homology and Cohomology

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                | Location                                                                          | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Earlier FC05 dependencies                                                           |
| --------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `FC05-C06-U001` | **Definition — Left G-module and G-mod**                                          | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4852                         | For a group G, a left G-module is an abelian group A with an action of G by additive automorphisms. G-mod identifies with left ZG-modules and with functors from the one-object category G to Ab.                                                    | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U002` | **Definition — Trivial G-module, invariants, and coinvariants**                   | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4854–4858                    | The exact trivial-module functor Ab→G-mod sends A to the zero-action module. For a G-module A, A^G consists of fixed points and A_G=A/⟨ga-a⟩ is the coinvariant quotient.                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U003` | **Lemma 6.1.1 — Invariants and coinvariants as Hom/tensor**                       | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4866                         | For the trivial G-module Z, A_G≅Z⊗_{ZG}A and A^G≅Hom_G(Z,A).                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U001`, `FC05-C06-U002`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U004` | **Definition 6.1.2 — Group homology and cohomology**                              | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4870                         | Define H_*(G;A)=L_*(-*G)(A)≅Tor**^{ZG}(Z,A) and H^*(G;A)=R^*(-^G)(A)≅Ext^*_{ZG}(Z,A); H_0=A_G and H^0=A^G.                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U003`, `FC05-C02-U038`, `FC05-C02-U047`, `FC05-C02-U056`, `FC05-C02-U048` |
| `FC05-C06-U005` | **Example 6.1.3 — Trivial group**                                                 | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4872                         | For G=1 the invariant and coinvariant functors are the identity, so H_n(1;A)=H^n(1;A)=0 for n>0.                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U004`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U006` | **Example 6.1.4 — Infinite cyclic group**                                         | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4874–4886                    | For T=⟨t⟩≅Z, the resolution 0→ZT→^{t-1}ZT→Z→0 computes H_0(T;A)=A/(t-1)A, H_1(T;A)=A^T, H^0(T;A)=A^T, H^1(T;A)=A/(t-1)A, with higher groups zero.                                                                                                    | `FC05-C06-U004`, `FC05-C02-U015`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U007` | **Definition 6.1.5 — Augmentation ideal**                                         | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4900                         | The augmentation ε:ZG→Z sends Σn_g g to Σn_g; its kernel I is the augmentation ideal, generated as an abelian group by g-1.                                                                                                                          | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U008` | **Example 6.1.6 — Coinvariants via the augmentation ideal**                       | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4902                         | Since Z≅ZG/I, H_0(G;A)=A_G≅A/IA; in particular H_0(G;Z)=Z.                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U003`, `FC05-C06-U007`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U009` | **Example 6.1.7 — Regular module coefficients**                                   | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4904–4906                    | Because ZG is projective over itself, H_n(G;ZG)=0 for n>0 and H_0(G;ZG)=Z; for finite G the corresponding positive-degree cohomology vanishing follows from Shapiro later.                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U004`, `FC05-C02-U012`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U010` | **Definition 6.1.8 — Norm element**                                               | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4908                         | For finite G, N=Σ_{g∈G}g∈ZG is central and G-invariant.                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U011` | **Lemma 6.1.9 — Invariants of the regular module**                                | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4910                         | For finite G, (ZG)^G is the two-sided ideal Z·N generated by the norm.                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC05-C06-U010`, `FC05-C06-U002`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U012` | **Proposition 6.1.10 — Vanishing when \|G\| is invertible**                       | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4920–4924                    | If \|G\|=m acts invertibly on A, e=N/m is a central idempotent and A_G≅A^G≅eA; all H_n(G;A),H^n(G;A) vanish for n>0.                                                                                                                                 | `FC05-C06-U010`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U013` | **Theorem 6.1.11 — H_1 and abelianization**                                       | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4948                         | For every group G, H_1(G;Z)≅I/I^2≅G/[G,G].                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U007`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U014` | **Theorem 6.1.12 — Trivial-coefficient universal coefficient formulas**           | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4958–4962                    | For a trivial G-module A, H_1(G;A)≅G_ab⊗A, and in higher degrees the universal coefficient theorem expresses H_n(G;A) and H^n(G;A) from H_*(G;Z), tensor, Tor, and Ext over Z.                                                                       | `FC05-C06-U013`, `FC05-C03-U061`, `FC05-C03-U064`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U015` | **Proposition 6.1.13 — Homology of a product of groups**                          | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4980–4984                    | For groups G,H, the Künneth theorem gives the natural split short exact sequence expressing H_n(G×H;Z) by tensor products and Tor_1 of H_*(G;Z),H_*(H;Z).                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U004`, `FC05-C03-U060`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U016` | **Construction 6.1.14 — Cohomological cross product**                             | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L4996–5006                    | Projective resolutions for G and H induce a natural cross product H^p(G;Z)⊗H^q(H;Z)→H^{p+q}(G×H;Z), compatible with tensor-product total complexes.                                                                                                  | `FC05-C06-U004`, `FC05-C06-U015`, `FC05-C01-U016`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U017` | **Definition 6.1.15 — Group hyperhomology and hypercohomology**                   | §6.1 Definitions and First Properties; pp. 160–166; L5034–5048                    | For a chain complex of G-modules A_*, write 𝕳_i(G;A_*) for the hyper-derived functors of coinvariants; dually 𝕳^i(G;A^*) denotes hypercohomology of invariants.                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U002`, `FC05-C05-U054`, `FC05-C05-U059`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U018` | **Construction 6.2.1 — Periodic resolution of a cyclic group**                    | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5062–5074                              | For C_m=⟨σ⟩, (σ-1)N=N(σ-1)=0 and the alternating maps σ-1,N give a 2-periodic free ZC_m-resolution of Z.                                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U010`, `FC05-C06-U007`, `FC05-C02-U015`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U019` | **Theorem 6.2.2 — Homology and cohomology of a cyclic group**                     | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5076–5084                              | Applying tensor or Hom to the periodic resolution gives the standard 2-periodic formulas: odd/even positive degrees alternate between invariants modulo norms and norm-kernel modulo (σ-1), with the cohomological parity reversed accordingly.      | `FC05-C06-U018`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U020` | **Example 6.2.3 — Cyclic group with trivial integer coefficients**                | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5092–5100                              | For C_m with trivial Z coefficients, H_{2i+1}(C_m;Z)≅Z/m, H_{2i}(C_m;Z)=0 for i>0, while H^{2i}(C_m;Z)≅Z/m for i>0 and odd cohomology vanishes.                                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U019`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U021` | **Definition 6.2.4 — Tate cohomology for cyclic groups**                          | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5104–5114                              | Extend cyclic cohomology to all integer degrees by hat H^{2i}(C_m;A)=A^G/NA and hat H^{2i+1}(C_m;A)=ker N/(σ-1)A.                                                                                                                                    | `FC05-C06-U019`, `FC05-C06-U010`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U022` | **Example 6.2.5 — Dimension shifting for Tate cohomology**                        | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5122                                   | A short exact sequence 0→K→P→A→0 with P projective yields hat H^n(G;A)≅hat H^{n+1}(G;K), since Tate cohomology of projectives vanishes.                                                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U021`, `FC05-C02-U016`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U023` | **Proposition 6.2.6 — Augmentation ideal of a free group**                        | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5124                                   | If G is free on X, its augmentation ideal I⊂ZG is a free ZG-module with basis {x-1:x∈X}.                                                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U007`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U024` | **Corollary 6.2.7 — Cohomological dimension of a free group**                     | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5134–5144                              | For a free group G, 0→I→ZG→Z→0 is a free resolution, so H_n(G;A)=H^n(G;A)=0 for n≥2 and pd_{ZG}Z=1.                                                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C06-U023`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U025` | **Construction 6.2.8 — Free product of groups**                                   | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5155                                   | The free product G*H is the coproduct of groups; every nonidentity element has a unique reduced alternating word in nonidentity elements of G and H.                                                                                                 | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U026` | **Proposition 6.2.9 — Augmentation ideal of a free product**                      | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5157–5161                              | For Λ=Z(G*H), I_{G*H}≅(I_G⊗*{ZG}Λ)⊕(I_H⊗*{ZH}Λ) as Λ-modules.                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C06-U025`, `FC05-C06-U007`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U027` | **Corollary 6.2.10 — Higher (co)homology of a free product**                      | §6.2 Cyclic and Free Groups; pp. 167–170; L5177–5187                              | For any (G*H)-module A and n≥2, H_n(G*H;A)≅H_n(G;A)⊕H_n(H;A), and similarly for cohomology.                                                                                                                                                          | `FC05-C06-U026`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U028` | **Definition 6.3.1 — Induced and coinduced modules**                              | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5213                                          | For H≤G and an H-module A, Ind_H^G A=ZG⊗_{ZH}A and Coind_H^G A=Hom_H(ZG,A), with their natural G-actions.                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U029` | **Theorem 6.3.2 — Shapiro's lemma**                                               | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5215–5219                                     | For H≤G, H_*(G;Ind_H^G A)≅H_*(H;A) and H^*(G;Coind_H^G A)≅H^*(H;A).                                                                                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C06-U028`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U030` | **Corollary 6.3.3 — Shapiro for the trivial subgroup**                            | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5245–5249                                     | For an abelian group A, induced/coinduced modules from H=1 have group (co)homology A in degree zero and vanish in positive degrees.                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C06-U029`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C06-U032` | **Corollary 6.3.5 — Finite-group cohomology of induced modules**                  | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5261                                          | For finite G, H^n(G;ZG⊗_Z A)=0 for n>0.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U030`, `FC05-C06-U031`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U033` | **Corollary 6.3.6 — Tate cohomology of projectives**                              | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5263–5267                                     | For finite G and projective G-module P, hat H^n(G;P)=0 in every integer degree.                                                                                                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U021`, `FC05-C06-U032`                                                    |

| `FC05-C06-U035` | **Example 6.3.8 — Cyclic additive Hilbert 90**                                    | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5279–5293                                     | For cyclic L/K with generator σ, the periodic cyclic calculation identifies trace with the norm element and gives exactness of the alternating σ-1 and trace maps on L.                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U019`, `FC05-C06-U034`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U036` | **Example 6.3.9 — Transfer at finite index**                                      | §6.3 Shapiro's Lemma; pp. 171–173; L5295                                          | For H≤G of finite index, the natural map from a G-module A into Coind_H^G A≅Ind_H^G A induces transfer/corestriction maps in (co)homology; on degree zero these are the coset-sum/norm maps.                                                         | `FC05-C06-U028`, `FC05-C06-U031`, `FC05-C06-U029`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U037` | **Definition 6.4.1 — Derivation / crossed homomorphism**                          | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5301                            | A derivation D:G→A into a left G-module satisfies D(gh)=gD(h)+D(g); derivations form an abelian group Der(G,A).                                                                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U038` | **Example 6.4.2 — Principal derivations**                                         | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5303–5309                       | Each a∈A defines D_a(g)=ga-a; these form the subgroup PDer(G,A)⊂Der(G,A).                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U037`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U039` | **Example 6.4.3 — Derivations from augmentation-ideal maps**                      | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5313–5317                       | A G-map φ:I→A determines D_φ(g)=φ(g-1), a derivation.                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC05-C06-U007`, `FC05-C06-U037`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U040` | **Lemma 6.4.4 — Augmentation ideal represents derivations**                       | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5319–5323                       | The map φ↦D_φ is a natural isomorphism Hom_G(I,A)≅Der(G,A).                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC05-C06-U039`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U041` | **Theorem 6.4.5 — H^1 as derivations modulo principal derivations**               | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5333                            | There is a natural isomorphism H^1(G;A)≅Der(G,A)/PDer(G,A).                                                                                                                                                                                          | `FC05-C06-U040`, `FC05-C06-U038`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U042` | **Corollary 6.4.6 — H^1 with trivial coefficients**                               | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5343–5347                       | If A is trivial, H^1(G;A)≅Der(G,A)≅Hom_Grp(G,A), equivalently Hom(G_ab,A).                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U041`, `FC05-C06-U002`, `FC05-C06-U013`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U043` | **Theorem 6.4.7 — Multiplicative Hilbert 90**                                     | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5351                            | For a finite Galois extension L/K with group G, the G-module L^× satisfies H^1(G;L^×)=0.                                                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U041`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U044` | **Example 6.4.8 — Cyclic multiplicative Hilbert 90**                              | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5355–5363                       | For cyclic L/K, H^1(G;L^×)=0 says every norm-one element has the form σ(b)/b; the degree-zero Tate quotient records K^×/N_{L/K}(L^×).                                                                                                                | `FC05-C06-U043`, `FC05-C06-U021`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U045` | **Construction — Semidirect product A⋊G**                                         | §6.4 Crossed Homomorphisms and H^1; pp. 174–176; L5365–5371                       | For a G-module A, A×G becomes a group with (a,g)(b,h)=(a+gb,gh); this semidirect product is a split extension of G by A.                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                     |

Then C_m⋊C_2 is the dihedral group D_m; the corresponding H^1 calculation measures stabilizing automorphisms via derivations modulo principal ones.                                                                 | `FC05-C06-U045`, `FC05-C06-U041`, `FC05-C06-U019`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U048` | **Construction — Normalized and unnormalized bar resolutions**                    | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5387–5393                                  | Define canonical free ZG-resolutions B_* and B_*^u of the trivial module Z, with basis symbols [g_1\|…\|g_n] (normalized symbols omit entries equal to 1).                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U004`, `FC05-C02-U015`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U049` | **Definition 6.5.1 — Bar differential**                                           | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5395–5405                                  | On B_n^u, d=Σ_{i=0}^n(-1)^i d_i, where d_0 uses the G-action on the first entry, interior faces multiply adjacent group elements, and d_n drops the final entry.                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U048`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C06-U051` | **Examples 6.5.2 — Low bar differentials**                                        | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5409–5421                                  | The bar differential gives d[g]=(g-1)[ ], d[g\|h]=g[h]-[gh]+[g], and the analogous alternating formula in degree three; its degree-one image is the augmentation ideal.                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U049`, `FC05-C06-U007`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U052` | **Theorem 6.5.3 — Exactness of the bar resolutions**                              | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5429                                       | Both normalized and unnormalized bar complexes are exact augmented complexes of free ZG-modules, hence free resolutions of Z.                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C06-U048`, `FC05-C06-U049`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U053` | **Example 6.5.4 — Homology from the bar resolution**                              | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5439–5443                                  | For a right G-module A, H_*(G;A) is the homology of A⊗*{ZG}B** (and analogously for left modules using the mirrored resolution).                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U052`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                                    |

| `FC05-C06-U055` | **Example 6.5.6 — Low-dimensional bar cohomology**                                | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5453–5455                                  | Degree-zero cocycles are A^G; one-coboundaries are principal derivations and one-cocycles are derivations, recovering H^1(G;A)=Der/PDer.                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U054`, `FC05-C06-U041`                                                    |

| `FC05-C06-U061` | **Example 6.5.12 — Low shuffle products**                                         | §6.5 The Bar Resolution; pp. 177–181; L5503–5507                                  | For abelian G, the shuffle product begins [g]*[h]=[g\|h]-[h\|g] and extends by the signed shuffle rule.                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U060`                                                                     |
