| `FC05-C06-U116` | **Corollary 6.9.9 — Vanishing Schur multiplier splits central extensions**        | §6.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 198–202; L6057–6061                        | If G is perfect and H_2(G;Z)=0, every central extension of G is a direct product with its abelian kernel.                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U112`, `FC05-C06-U114`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U117` | **Example 6.9.10 — Alternating groups and Schur multipliers**                     | §6.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 198–202; L6065–6083                        | For n≥5 the alternating groups are perfect; their H_2(A_n;Z) (Schur multipliers) determine the kernels of their universal central extensions, with the listed exceptional values for small n.                                                        | `FC05-C06-U112`, `FC05-C06-U107`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U118` | **Example 6.9.11 — Special linear groups**                                        | §6.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 198–202; L6085–6093                        | Except for the stated small finite-field cases, SL_n(F) is perfect. Its center is μ_n(F), so PSL_n(F) is a central quotient; universal-cover behavior is governed by H_2.                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U107`, `FC05-C06-U112`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U119` | **Example 6.9.12 — Elementary matrix groups**                                     | §6.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 198–202; L6095                             | For n≥3, the elementary subgroup E_n(R)⊂GL_n(R) generated by elementary matrices is perfect, using commutator identities among elementary matrices.                                                                                                  | `FC05-C06-U107`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U120` | **Definition 6.9.13 — Steinberg group**                                           | §6.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 198–202; L6097–6103                        | For n≥3, St_n(R) is generated by x_{ij}^λ with the additive and Steinberg commutator relations mirroring elementary matrices; it maps naturally onto E_n(R).                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U119`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U121` | **Definition — Proper action used for covering spaces**                           | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6107                             | A group action on a topological space X is proper here when every point has an open neighborhood U disjoint from each nontrivial translate gU; translations are assumed continuous.                                                                  | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U122` | **Example 6.10.1 — Universal-covering action**                                    | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6109                             | If Y is connected and locally simply connected, G=π_1(Y) acts properly by deck transformations on the universal cover X=Ỹ and X/G≅Y.                                                                                                                 | `FC05-C06-U121`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U123` | **Lemma 6.10.2 — Singular chains under a proper action**                          | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6111                             | If G acts properly on X, the singular chain complex S_*(X) is degreewise free over ZG and its coinvariant complex is naturally S_*(X/G).                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U121`, `FC05-C06-U001`, `FC05-C01-U002`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U124` | **Corollary 6.10.3 — G-action on singular (co)homology**                          | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6117                             | A proper G-action on X induces G-module structures on H_*(X;Z) and H^*(X;Z).                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U123`, `FC05-C06-U001`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U125` | **Definition 6.10.4 — Classifying space BG and universal space EG**               | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6119–6125                        | A CW complex BG with π_1(BG)=G and contractible universal cover is a classifying space for G; its universal cover is denoted EG.                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U122`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U126` | **Theorem 6.10.5 — Group (co)homology equals (co)homology of BG**                 | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6127                             | For any classifying space BG, H_*(BG;Z)≅H_*(G;Z) and H^*(BG;Z)≅H^*(G;Z).                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U125`, `FC05-C06-U123`, `FC05-C06-U004`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U127` | **Example 6.10.6 — Models for BZ**                                                | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6141                             | S^1 and C^× are models for BZ; their universal covers R and C are contractible with quotient maps given by the standard exponential/translation coverings.                                                                                           | `FC05-C06-U125`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U128` | **Example 6.10.7 — Model for BC_2**                                               | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6143                             | S^∞ is contractible with the free antipodal C_2-action, so RP^∞=S^∞/C_2 is a model for BC_2.                                                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U125`, `FC05-C06-U121`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U129` | **Example 6.10.8 — Aspherical Riemann surfaces**                                  | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6145                             | For an orientable surface S of genus g>0 with the standard one-relator presentation of π_1(S), its contractible universal cover makes S a model for Bπ_1(S).                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U125`, `FC05-C06-U122`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U130` | **Example 6.10.9 — Locally symmetric classifying spaces**                         | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6147–6149                        | If L is connected with maximal compact K and Γ≤L is discrete torsion-free, X=L/K is contractible and Γ acts properly, so Γ∖X is a model for BΓ.                                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U121`, `FC05-C06-U125`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U131` | **Theorem 6.10.10 — Equivariant-to-quotient spectral sequences**                  | §6.10 Covering Spaces in Topology; pp. 203–205; L6151–6155                        | If G acts properly on connected X and A is abelian, there are convergent spectral sequences H_p(G;H_q(X;A))⇒H_{p+q}(X/G;A) and H^p(G;H^q(X;A))⇒H^{p+q}(X/G;A).                                                                                       | `FC05-C06-U123`, `FC05-C06-U017`, `FC05-C05-U056`, `FC05-C05-U059`                  |
| `FC05-C06-U132` | **Krull Theorem 6.11.1 — Absolute Galois group as inverse limit**                 | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6189                  | For a field K with separable closure K_s, Gal(K_s/K) is naturally the inverse limit of the finite Galois groups Gal(L/K) over finite Galois subextensions L/K.                                                                                       | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U133` | **Example 6.11.2 — Absolute Galois group of a finite field**                      | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6193                  | For F_q, finite subextensions are F_{q^n} ordered by divisibility and Gal(overline F_q/F_q) is the inverse limit of the cyclic groups Z/n, i.e. the profinite completion hat Z.                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U132`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U134` | **Definition — Profinite topology on an inverse limit**                           | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6195                  | Give each finite set/group the discrete topology and an inverse limit the subspace topology from the product; this compact Hausdorff topology is the profinite topology.                                                                             | `FC05-C06-U132`, `FC05-C02-U063`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U135` | **Definition 6.11.3 — Profinite set**                                             | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6197–6199             | A profinite set is an inverse limit of finite sets equipped with the profinite topology; the chosen presentation is not part of the data.                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U134`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U136` | **Definition — Totally disconnected space**                                       | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6201                  | A topological space is totally disconnected when all connected components are singletons.                                                                                                                                                            | —                                                                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U137` | **Theorem 6.11.4 — Characterization of profinite spaces**                         | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6205                  | A space is profinite iff it is compact, Hausdorff, and totally disconnected. Hence the profinite structure depends only on the topology, not on a chosen inverse-limit presentation.                                                                 | `FC05-C06-U135`, `FC05-C06-U136`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U138` | **Definition 6.11.5 — Profinite group**                                           | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6211–6213             | A profinite group is an inverse limit of finite groups with its profinite topology; equivalently it is a compact Hausdorff totally disconnected topological group.                                                                                   | `FC05-C06-U134`, `FC05-C06-U137`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U139` | **Examples 6.11.6 — Profinite groups and completion**                             | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6215–6220             | Finite groups, p-adic integers, and absolute Galois groups are profinite. The profinite completion of an abstract group is the inverse limit of its finite quotients.                                                                                | `FC05-C06-U138`, `FC05-C06-U132`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U140` | **Lemma 6.11.7 — Open normal subgroups recover a profinite group**                | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6230                  | For profinite G, open normal subgroups form a neighborhood basis of 1, every quotient G/U is finite, and the canonical map G→lim_U G/U is an isomorphism of profinite groups.                                                                        | `FC05-C06-U138`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U141` | **Definition 6.11.8 — Discrete module over a profinite group**                    | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6240                  | A discrete G-module is a G-module A with A given the discrete topology such that G×A→A is continuous; equivalently each element has an open stabilizer.                                                                                              | `FC05-C06-U138`, `FC05-C06-U001`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U142` | **Examples 6.11.9 — Discrete Galois modules and finite groups**                   | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6248                  | K_s is a discrete Gal(K_s/K)-module; if G is finite, every ordinary G-module is discrete.                                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U132`, `FC05-C06-U141`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U143` | **Convention — Category C_G of discrete G-modules**                               | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6250                  | Let C_G be the full additive category of discrete G-modules with ordinary G-linear maps; it is an abelian subcategory of G-mod.                                                                                                                      | `FC05-C06-U141`, `FC05-C06-U001`, `FC05-C01-U013`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U144` | **Lemma 6.11.10 — Enough injectives for discrete profinite modules**              | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6260                  | The abelian category C_G of discrete G-modules has enough injective objects.                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C06-U143`, `FC05-C02-U021`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U145` | **Remark — C_G need not have enough projectives**                                 | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6264                  | In contrast with ordinary modules over a group ring, the category C_G of discrete profinite G-modules generally does not have enough projectives.                                                                                                    | `FC05-C06-U143`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C06-U146` | **Definition 6.11.11 — Continuous cohomology of a profinite group**               | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6266–6276             | For profinite G and discrete G-module A, define H^*(G;A) as the right derived functors in C_G of invariants A↦A^G. For finite G this agrees with ordinary group cohomology.                                                                          | `FC05-C06-U143`, `FC05-C06-U144`, `FC05-C06-U002`, `FC05-C02-U047`                  |
| `FC05-C06-U147` | **Definition 6.11.12 — Continuous cochains**                                      | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6278                  | For discrete A, C^n(G,A) is the abelian group of continuous maps G^n→A, with the usual inhomogeneous group-cochain differential.                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U141`, `FC05-C06-U054`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U148` | **Theorem 6.11.13 — Continuous cochain and finite-quotient formulas**             | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6286–6292             | For profinite G and discrete A, H^*(G;A)≅H^*(C^*(G,A))≅colim_U H^*(G/U;A^U), over open normal U.                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U146`, `FC05-C06-U147`, `FC05-C06-U140`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U149` | **Corollary 6.11.14 — Positive continuous cohomology is torsion**                 | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6316                  | For n≥1 and discrete coefficients A, H^n(G;A) is a torsion abelian group.                                                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C06-U148`, `FC05-C06-U057`                                                    |
| `FC05-C06-U150` | **Low Dimensions 6.11.15 — Continuous H^1 and H^2**                               | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6326                  | H^1(G;A) is continuous derivations modulo continuous principal derivations; H^2(G;A) consists of continuous factor-set classes and, for finite A, classifies profinite extensions of G by A.                                                         | `FC05-C06-U147`, `FC05-C06-U041`, `FC05-C06-U067`, `FC05-C06-U071`                  |
| `FC05-C06-U151` | **Hilbert's Theorem 90 6.11.16 — Absolute Galois cohomology**                     | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6328–6330             | For G=Gal(K_s/K), K_s and K_s^× are discrete G-modules with invariants K and K^×; H^n(G;K_s)=0 for n>0 and H^1(G;K_s^×)=0, obtained as direct limits of finite Galois Hilbert-90 calculations.                                                       | `FC05-C06-U142`, `FC05-C06-U148`, `FC05-C06-U034`, `FC05-C06-U043`                  |
| `FC05-C06-U152` | **Brauer Group 6.11.17 — Brauer group as H^2**                                    | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6340                  | Br(K) is the group of Brauer classes of finite-dimensional central simple K-algebras (equivalently division-algebra classes). It is the union of Br(L/K), hence Br(K)≅H^2(Gal(K_s/K);K_s^×).                                                         | `FC05-C06-U075`, `FC05-C06-U148`, `FC05-C06-U151`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U153` | **Proposition 6.11.18 — Relative Brauer exact sequence**                          | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6342–6348             | For a Galois extension L/K with group G, the low-degree Hochschild–Serre sequence gives 0→Br(L/K)→Br(K)→Br(L)^G→H^3(G;L^×)→H^3(K;K_s^×); in particular Br(L/K)=ker(Br(K)→Br(L)).                                                                     | `FC05-C06-U152`, `FC05-C06-U099`, `FC05-C06-U151`                                   |
| `FC05-C06-U154` | **Vista 6.11.19 — Standard Brauer-group comparisons**                             | §6.11 Galois Cohomology and Profinite Groups; pp. 206–215; L6360–6366             | Records cohomological consequences including Tsen's vanishing for one-variable function fields over algebraically closed fields, Br(R)≅Z/2, Br(K)≅Q/Z for local fields, and the global Hasse-invariant exact relation for Br(Q).                     | `FC05-C06-U152`, `FC05-C06-U153`                                                    |

## Chapter 7. Lie Algebra Homology and Cohomology

| ID              | Kind / source unit                                                                        | Location                                                             | Concise data                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | Earlier FC05 dependencies                                                                            |
| --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `FC05-C07-U001` | **Definition — Lie algebra over a commutative ring**                                      | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6378–6382                           | Fix a commutative ring k. A Lie algebra is a nonassociative k-algebra with a bilinear bracket. After the word “satisfies,” the extraction preserves the displayed Jacobi identity but does not preserve any companion defining axiom; no absent condition is reconstructed here.                         | —                                                                                                    |
| `FC05-C07-U002` | **Definition — Lie ideal, quotient, and Lie morphism**                                    | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6384–6388                           | A Lie ideal h⊂g is a k-submodule with [g,h]⊂h; g/h inherits a Lie bracket, Lie morphisms are bracket-preserving k-linear maps, and 0→h→g→g/h→0 is exact.                                                                                                                                                 | `FC05-C07-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U003` | **Example 7.1.1 — Abelian Lie algebras**                                                  | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6390                                | A Lie algebra is abelian when every bracket vanishes; every k-module has this zero-bracket Lie algebra structure.                                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C07-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U004` | **Construction — Commutator ideal and abelianization**                                    | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6392                                | [g,g] is the ideal generated by all brackets and g^ab=g/[g,g] is the largest abelian quotient Lie algebra.                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC05-C07-U002`, `FC05-C07-U003`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U005` | **Example 7.1.2 — Lie algebra underlying an associative algebra**                         | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6394                                | For an associative k-algebra A, the commutator [x,y]=xy-yx makes the underlying k-module a Lie algebra Lie(A), functorially in A.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C07-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U006` | **Examples 7.1.3 — Matrix Lie algebras**                                                  | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6396–6409                           | For an associative k-algebra A, gl_m(A)=Lie(M_m(A)); standard Lie subalgebras include the orthogonal, special-linear, upper-triangular, and strictly-upper-triangular algebras, with sl_m(A)=ker(tr:gl_m(A)→A/[A,A]).                                                                                    | `FC05-C07-U005`, `FC05-C07-U004`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U007` | **Example 7.1.4 — Derivation Lie algebra**                                                | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6411–6423                           | For a nonassociative k-algebra A, derivations D satisfying D(ab)=D(a)b+aD(b) form a Lie subalgebra Der(A)⊂Lie(End_k(A)) under the commutator.                                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C07-U005`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U008` | **Example 7.1.5 — Free Lie algebra**                                                      | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6425–6439                           | The free Lie algebra f(M) on a k-module M contains M and represents k-linear maps from M to Lie algebras: Hom_k(M,g)≅Hom_Lie(f(M),g); similarly for a free Lie algebra on a set.                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C07-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U009` | **Definition 7.1.6 — Lower central series and nilpotence**                                | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6451–6457                           | Define g^2=[g,g] and g^n=[g^{n-1},g]. The Lie algebra is nilpotent when g^n=0 for some n; strictly upper-triangular matrix Lie algebras are examples.                                                                                                                                                    | `FC05-C07-U004`, `FC05-C07-U006`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U010` | **Definition 7.1.7 — Derived series and solvability**                                     | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6459–6465                           | Define g'=[g,g] and g^{(n)}=[g^{(n-1)},g^{(n-1)}]. The Lie algebra is solvable when this derived series reaches zero.                                                                                                                                                                                    | `FC05-C07-U004`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U011` | **Lemma 7.1.8 — Nilpotent implies solvable**                                              | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6467                                | Every nilpotent Lie algebra is solvable; the proof uses [g^i,g^j]⊂g^{i+j} to compare the derived and lower-central series.                                                                                                                                                                               | `FC05-C07-U009`, `FC05-C07-U010`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U012` | **Example 7.1.9 — Upper-triangular Lie algebra**                                          | §7.1 Lie Algebras; pp. 216–218; L6475                                | For commutative A, the upper-triangular Lie algebra t_m(A) is solvable but not nilpotent.                                                                                                                                                                                                                | `FC05-C07-U006`, `FC05-C07-U009`, `FC05-C07-U010`                                                    |
| `FC05-C07-U013` | **Definition — Left g-module**                                                            | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6479–6483                              | A left g-module is a k-module M with a bilinear action g⊗M→M satisfying [x,y]m=x(ym)-y(xm).                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C07-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U014` | **Examples 7.2.1 — Standard g-modules**                                                   | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6485–6489                              | An A-module is a Lie(A)-module; g acts on itself by the adjoint representation; and any k-module becomes a trivial g-module by zero action.                                                                                                                                                              | `FC05-C07-U005`, `FC05-C07-U013`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U015` | **Definition — g-module maps and g-mod**                                                  | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6491–6493                              | A g-module morphism is a k-linear map commuting with the g-action. These form Hom_g(M,N), and left g-modules form the additive category g-mod.                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C07-U013`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C07-U016` | **Construction — Trivial modules, invariants, and coinvariants**                          | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6503–6513                              | The trivial-module functor k-mod→g-mod is exact. For a g-module M, the main text defines M^g={m:xm=0 for all x}≅Hom_g(k,M) and M_g=M/gM. The adjunction and one-sided exactness conclusions assigned to Exercise 7.2.4 are not promoted.                                                                 | `FC05-C07-U013`, `FC05-C07-U014`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U017` | **Definition 7.2.2 — Lie algebra homology and cohomology**                                | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6522–6524                              | Define H_*^{Lie}(g,M)=L_*(-*g)(M), with H_0=M_g, and H^**{Lie}(g,M)=R^*(-^g)(M), with H^0=M^g.                                                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C07-U016`, `FC05-C02-U038`, `FC05-C02-U047`                                                    |
| `FC05-C07-U018` | **Examples 7.2.3 — Basic Lie (co)homology calculations**                                  | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6526–6544                              | For g=0 all higher groups vanish. For a finite-rank abelian g, Lie (co)homology identifies with Tor/Ext over the polynomial enveloping ring; for a free Lie algebra on X it identifies with Tor/Ext over the free associative ring k{X}.                                                                 | `FC05-C07-U017`, `FC05-C07-U003`, `FC05-C07-U008`, `FC05-C02-U056`, `FC05-C02-U048`                  |
| `FC05-C07-U019` | **Proposition 7.2.4 — Free-ring augmentation ideal resolution**                           | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6546–6552                              | For R=k{X}, the augmentation ideal I=Xk{X} is free as a right R-module on X, so 0→I→R→k→0 is a free resolution of k.                                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C07-U018`, `FC05-C02-U015`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U020` | **Corollary 7.2.5 — (Co)homology of a free Lie algebra**                                  | §7.2 g-Modules; pp. 219–222; L6556–6560                              | For the free Lie algebra f(X), H_n(f,M)=H^n(f,M)=0 for n≥2. With trivial coefficients k, H_1(f,k)≅⊕*{x∈X}k and H^1(f,k)≅∏*{x∈X}k.                                                                                                                                                                        | `FC05-C07-U018`, `FC05-C07-U019`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U021` | **Definition 7.3.1 — Tensor algebra**                                                     | §7.3 Universal Enveloping Algebras; pp. 223–227; L6572–6584          | For a k-module M, T(M) is the graded unital associative k-algebra generated by M; the surviving text describes its tensor-power pieces M^{⊗n} and multiplication by concatenation, and says that for free M it is the free associative algebra on a basis. | `FC05-C07-U077` | **Lemma 7.9.9 — Normalized cocycles are Killing-form valued**                             | §7.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 248–253; L7496–7502           | Over k=C, for a normalized splitting σ every coefficient bilinear form f_σ(x t^i,y t^j) is g-invariant, hence equals κ(x,y)c_{ij} for some c_{ij} in the central kernel.                                                                                                                                 | `FC05-C07-U076`, `FC05-C07-U054`, `FC05-C07-U055`                                                    |
| `FC05-C07-U078` | **Corollary 7.9.10 — Normalized cocycles are multiples of β**                             | §7.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 248–253; L7518–7522           | Over C, the cocycle of a normalized splitting has the form f_σ(x,y)=β(x,y)c for a single central element c; equivalently c_{i,-i}=ic and c_{ij}=0 when i+j≠0.                                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C07-U077`, `FC05-C07-U074`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C07-U079` | **Theorem 7.9.11 (Garland) — Universal affine central extension**                         | §7.9 Universal Central Extensions; pp. 248–253; L7544                | For finite-dimensional simple g over C, the affine Lie algebra ĝ of 7.9.6 is the universal central extension of g[t,t^{-1}].                                                                                                                                                                             | `FC05-C07-U075`, `FC05-C07-U076`, `FC05-C07-U078`, `FC05-C07-U070`                                   |

Over subfields, the kernel dimension reflects the number of simple factors after complexification.                                                         | `FC05-C07-U079`, `FC05-C07-U056`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C08-U001` | Definition — simplicial category Δ; simplicial and cosimplicial objects                   | §8.1 Simplicial Objects; pp. 254–258; L7570                    | Let Δ be the category of finite ordinals [n]={0<1<…<n} and monotone maps. A simplicial object in C is a contravariant functor Δ→C; a cosimplicial object is a covariant functor Δ→C.                                                                                                                                       | —                                                                                                                     |
| `FC05-C08-U002` | Example 8.1.1                                                                             | §8.1 Simplicial Objects; pp. 254–258; L7572                    | For any object A of a category C, the constant functor Δ^op→C with value A is the constant simplicial object at A.                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `FC05-C08-U001`                                                                                                       |
| `FC05-C08-U003` | Lemma 8.1.2                                                                               | §8.1 Simplicial Objects; pp. 254–258; L7588                    | Every monotone map [n]→[m] factors uniquely as a surjection followed by an injection; equivalently it has unique expressions in the standard degeneracy and face maps with the prescribed index orders.                                                                                                                    | `FC05-C08-U001`                                                                                                       |
| `FC05-C08-U004` | Proposition 8.1.3                                                                         | §8.1 Simplicial Objects; pp. 254–258; L7608                    | A simplicial object is equivalently a sequence A_n with face maps ∂*i:A_n→A*{n-1} and degeneracy maps σ_i:A_n→A_{n+1} satisfying the standard simplicial identities.                                                                                                                                                       | `FC05-C08-U001`, `FC05-C08-U003`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C08-U005` | Corollary 8.1.4                                                                           | §8.1 Simplicial Objects; pp. 254–258; L7628                    | Dually, a cosimplicial object is equivalently a sequence A^n with coface maps ∂^i and codegeneracy maps σ^i satisfying the dual simplicial identities.                                                                                                                                                                     | `FC05-C08-U001`, `FC05-C08-U004`                                                                                      |

| `FC05-C08-U058` | Definition 8.6.1                                                                          | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8169                 | A triple (monad) on a category C is an endofunctor T with unit η:id→T and multiplication μ:T^2→T satisfying associativity and unit laws; a cotriple (comonad) is the dual structure.                                                                                                                                       | `FC05-C01-U009`                                                                                                       |

| `FC05-C08-U060` | Example 8.6.3                                                                             | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8215                 | For G-modules, the adjunction ZG⊗_Z- ⊣ forgetful gives the standard free-module cotriple used to construct canonical group-homology resolutions.                                                                                                                                                                           | `FC05-C08-U059`, `FC05-C06-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C08-U061` | Construction — Simplicial Object of a Cotriple 8.6.4                                      | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8217                 | For a cotriple ⊥ with counit ε and comultiplication δ, define ⊥_nA=⊥^{n+1}A, with faces ∂_i=⊥^i ε ⊥^{n-i} and degeneracies σ_i=⊥^i δ ⊥^{n-i}. These maps form the canonical augmented simplicial object ⊥_*A→A. | `FC05-C08-U058`, `FC05-C08-U001`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C08-U062` | Definition 8.6.5                                                                          | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8239                 | For a cotriple ⊥ with counit ε, an object P is ⊥-projective when ε_P:⊥P→P admits a section. | `FC05-C08-U065` | Proposition 8.6.8                                                                         | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8249                 | The canonical augmented simplicial object ⊥_*A→A associated to a cotriple is aspherical for every ⊥-projective A; in an abelian category its associated augmented chain complex is exact.                                                                                                                                  | `FC05-C08-U061`, `FC05-C08-U062`, `FC05-C08-U049`                                                                     |
| `FC05-C08-U066` | Corollary 8.6.9                                                                           | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8263                 | If U:A→C has a left adjoint F and A is abelian, then for every C∈C the augmented simplicial object ⊥_*(FC)→FC is contractible, hence aspherical. | `FC05-C08-U059`, `FC05-C08-U065`                                                                                      |
| `FC05-C08-U067` | Proposition 8.6.10                                                                        | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8265                 | If U:A→C has a left adjoint F, then for every A∈A the augmented simplicial object U(⊥_*A)→U(A) is left contractible in C, hence aspherical. | `FC05-C08-U059`, `FC05-C08-U061`                                                                                      |

| `FC05-C08-U071` | Comparison — bar-resolution Eilenberg-Zilber homotopy equivalence                         | §8.6 Canonical Resolutions; pp. 278–285; L8303                 | The Eilenberg-Zilber/shuffle map Tot β(R,R)⊗β(R′,R′)→β(R⊗R′,R⊗R′) is a chain-homotopy equivalence; tensoring it with coefficients yields Proposition 8.6.13                                                                                                                                                                | `FC05-C08-U070`, `FC05-C08-U069`, `FC05-C08-U053`                                                                     |

| `FC05-C10-U001` | **Construction/comparison — two-stage construction of the derived category**             | Chapter 10 introduction; p. 369; L10676                                     | For an abelian category A, first pass from Ch(A) to K(A) by identifying chain-homotopic maps, then localize K(A) by inverting quasi-isomorphisms to obtain D(A).                                                                       | `FC05-C01-U004`, `FC05-C01-U035`                                   |
| `FC05-C10-U002` | **Definition — homotopy category K(A)**                                                  | §10.1 The Category K(A); pp. 369–372; L10680                                | K(A) has the same cochain complexes as Ch(A), with morphisms chain maps modulo chain homotopy; the quotient functor Ch(A)→K(A) is additive.                                                                                            | `FC05-C01-U005`, `FC05-C01-U035`, `FC05-C01-U009`                  |
| `FC05-C10-U003` | **Construction — quotient subcategory associated to C⊂Ch(A)**                            | §10.1 The Category K(A); pp. 369–372; L10682–10690                          | For a full subcategory C of Ch(A), define the corresponding full subcategory of K(A) on the same complexes; its Hom sets are Hom_C/∼, and it is additive when C is closed under ⊕ and contains 0.                                      | `FC05-C10-U002`, `FC05-C01-U010`                                   |

| `FC05-C10-U005` | **Lemma 10.1.1**                                                                         | §10.1 The Category K(A); pp. 369–372; L10698                                | Cohomology of cochain complexes descends through chain homotopy to well-defined functors H^i:K(A)→A.                                                                                                                                   | `FC05-C10-U002`, `FC05-C01-U005`                                   |
| `FC05-C10-U006` | **Proposition 10.1.2 (Universal property)**                                              | §10.1 The Category K(A); pp. 369–372; L10702–10705                          | Any functor F:Ch(A)→D that sends chain-homotopy equivalences to isomorphisms factors uniquely through K(A).                                                                                                                            | `FC05-C10-U002`, `FC05-C01-U035`                                   |
| `FC05-C10-U007` | **Definition 10.1.3 (Triangles in K(A))**                                                | §10.1 The Category K(A); pp. 369–372; L10726–10742                          | For u:A→B, the mapping-cone sequence defines a strict triangle; an exact triangle is a triangle isomorphic in K(A) to a strict triangle.                                                                                               | `FC05-C10-U002`, `FC05-C01-U038`, `FC05-C01-U021`                  |
